2645:\textbf{Bottom line.} Resolving the confound stated at the start of this section: the TSFM is dead weight on ETTh2, ETTm2, and Electricity, where the backbone-free Raw-MLP MoE wins by $0.7$--$17.9\%$ over Dual-Stream, and contributes $3$--$6\%$ MSE on ETTh1, ETTm1, and Weather, where Dual-Stream retains the advantage. The dataset-conditional contribution of the TSFM is itself an empirical pattern worth flagging to the TSFM-design community: \emph{frozen TSFMs are useful where forecasting requires nonlinear temporal modeling beyond what a 5-MLP ensemble can express, and unnecessary where it does not}. The single most important consequence for our paper is that the routing-collapse diagnosis (the core scientific contribution) is robust to whether the TSFM is the best backbone: it explains the AdaMix-collapse phenomenon and predicts the cross-backbone behavior regardless.

560	\label{thm:mi_decomp}
561	Let $X$ be a random input window, $E \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, $S = (X - M)/\Sigma$ the RevIN-normalized shape. \emph{(i) Exact decomposition:} $I(X;E) - I(S;E) = I(M,\Sigma;E\mid S) \geq 0$. \emph{(ii) Quantitative bound (no independence assumption):} $I(X;E) - I(S;E) \geq I(M,\Sigma;E) - I(M,\Sigma;S)$, with $\varepsilon := I(M,\Sigma;S) \geq 0$ capturing residual leakage of stripped statistics through the shape; equality iff $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid E$.
562	\end{observation}
563	
564	When routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$; Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X;E){-}I(S;E){=}H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. The signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M)+\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ is a tractable monotone proxy; the bound is empirically tight (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}) and learnable $\alpha{<}0.5$ on $30/30$ runs (Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), whereas $H(E\mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$); Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) is flagged a boundary, Solar ($R{=}0.06$) the one outlier (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).
565	
566	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins on every cell for instance-normalized backbones (MOMENT-small/large $26$--$79\%/32$--$75\%$, Moirai $12$--$29\%$, Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} $37$--$90\%$); LayerNorm-only controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}) show no collapse. Top-$2$, imputation $7/8$, $24/24$ horizons, and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} ($6{\times}5{\times}4$) confirm robustness (Tables~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}--\ref{tab:horizon}; Appendices~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation},~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}
567	
568	\vspace{-0.6em}
569	\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
570	\vspace{-0.6em}
571	
572	MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization handling distribution shift also strips per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ flags applicable settings (vision replication, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). \textbf{Limitations:} instance-level only; monotone $R(\mathcal{D})$; forecasting + imputation.\label{page:end_main}

81:\begin{figure}[t]
223:\caption{\textbf{Problem overview.} \textbf{(a)}~TSFM adapters use the same head for every window. \textbf{(b)}~The natural mixture-of-experts extension fails on instance-normalized TSFMs under unfreezing: entropy collapses ($0.000{\pm}0.000$) and the remaining experts receive no gradient. Same backbone, different heads.}
241:\begin{figure}[t!]
363:\caption{\textbf{RR-MoA architecture.} \textit{Path~A} (blue): frozen TSFM produces $\mathbf{H}$ for all experts. \textit{Path~B} (orange): Conv1d gate ($853$ params) reads $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ and selects Top-2 experts; inactive experts (gray) receive no gradient. Standard hidden-state MoE collapses (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}); RR-MoA wins \textbf{54/54} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}).}
405:\begin{table}[h]
407:\caption{\textbf{Freeze-level ablation} (test MSE, mean$\pm$std, 3 seeds $\{42,43,44\}$, H=96). RR-MoA (sparse Top-2 routing over 5 experts) vs.\ the best single-head adapter from $\{$linear, attention, conv$\}$ trained with the same protocol. ``MSE $\Delta\%$ vs.\ single-head'' is the relative MSE change of RR-MoA over the single-head baseline; negative $=$ lower MSE $=$ better. RR-MoA wins \textbf{27/27} paired (cell, seed) comparisons in this table; combined with Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa} the score is \textbf{54/54} across 6 datasets $\times$ 3 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds. The gain is largest when frozen (``Frozen Paradox'').}
432:\begin{table}[h]
434:\caption{\textbf{AdaMix routing collapse} (entropy max ${\approx}1.609$). Under unfreezing, entropy ${\to}0.000$. \colorbox{green!8}{Green}: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy ($0.66$--$1.32$), proving RevIN is the causal mechanism. Extended grid in Appendix~\ref{app:extended_rrmoa}.}
464:\begin{figure}[h]
466:\includegraphics[width=0.92\columnwidth]{figures/adamix_trajectory.pdf}
468:\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}40$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~The dominant expert (Attn) retains a gradient of ${\sim}10^{-2}$ throughout training, while the other four experts crash to the numerical noise floor (${<}10^{-3}$) by step 30 and never recover. Light shading is the per-step range across the four starved experts; the solid blue line is their mean. Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) inverts the trajectory.}
473:\begin{figure}[h]
475:\includegraphics[width=0.62\columnwidth]{figures/causal_contrast.pdf}
477:\caption{\textbf{Causal contrast: MOMENT vs Timer-XL} (ETTh1, last-4 unfreezing). Solid: unfrozen; MOMENT entropy collapses $0.51{\to}0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps; Timer-XL holds ${\approx}1.60$ for $400$ steps. Dashed: frozen controls. Dotted: $\log K{=}1.609$ ceiling. Same code, same data, only normalization differs.}
498:\begin{table}[h]
500:\caption{\textbf{Eight causal controls.} Each rules out a competing explanation; the only mechanism consistent with all 8 is that \emph{instance normalization strips the per-window $(\mu,\sigma)$ signal the router needs}.}
523:\begin{table}[h]
525:\caption{\textbf{Baseline comparison} (MOMENT-small, Top-2, 5 seeds, H=96). Above double rule: frozen; full FT unfreezes all 8 blocks. Wilcoxon, Bonferroni-corrected. Grey rows: from-scratch calibration anchors.}

481:In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $26\%$ to $79\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, consistent with Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction that low $R$ implies little benefit (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
483:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} The trajectory and causal-contrast figures formalize into a sharp prediction: if normalization-driven gradient co-adaptation is the collapse mechanism, then within a fixed MoE architecture, frozen should match or beat unfrozen. Empirically, frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}; the three-dataset subset in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} is consistent). The two-factor headline gap (frozen+MoE vs.\ unfrozen+single-head) decomposes into:

228	\section{Related Work}
229	
230	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data: LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; complementary work computes transferability scores for backbone selection (TEMPLATE,~\citealp{zhang2025template}) or provides a unified evaluation suite for general forecasting models (GIFT-Eval,~\citealp{woo2024gifteval}). These works largely vary the finetuning \textit{strategy} (where to inject parameters, what to prune, what to prompt, which transferability score to use) while leaving the per-input adapter \textit{topology} fixed; AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} proposes several adapter variants and reports the best per dataset/horizon, treating adapter choice as a per-task hyperparameter rather than routing per input window. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window via routing over heterogeneous experts; the closest precedent in spirit is mixture-of-adapters in the LLM setting (e.g., AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix}; AdaMix mixes copies of one adapter type, whereas RR-MoA uses $K$ topologically distinct expert architectures), whereas, in the TSFM literature we surveyed, we did not find prior work that uses a per-window mixture over distinct adapter topologies.
231	
232	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been used \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale capacity, with experts placed inside the backbone architecture: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes individual tokens to data-driven experts that automatically specialize across diverse time series patterns, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. RR-MoA applies MoE on an orthogonal axis, at the \emph{adapter level} on top of a frozen backbone. The two axes operate on different parts of the model: stacking RR-MoA on Moirai-MoE further improves accuracy on the datasets we tested (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
233	
234	\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Existing diagnoses of routing collapse cluster, broadly, into three threads. \emph{Optimization-side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, and AdaMix's hidden-state router~\citep{wang2022adamix} target training dynamics; \citet{guo2025expertspec} further argue that the standard load-balance loss itself causes expert overlap and propose orthogonality and variance regularizers. \emph{Representation-geometry}: \citet{chi2022representation} attribute collapse to token clustering around expert centroids that emerges \emph{during} routing learning and propose hyperspherical routing scores; \citet{wu2024mhmoe} split each token into sub-tokens to raise expert activation. \emph{Architectural / paradigm}: \citet{zhou2022expertchoice} invert the token--expert paradigm via Expert-Choice routing, \citet{wang2025remoe} replace top-$k$ with ReLU gating, and \citet{hua2025inputaware} decouple the router from the task objective so that routing decisions reflect input structure rather than task gradients. Our setting differs from all three: the routing signal is structurally absent from the router's input \emph{by design of an upstream architectural normalizer (RevIN)}, before training dynamics, geometry, or routing-paradigm choices can act. Sweeping five rescue families spanning the optimization and routing-paradigm threads (load balancing, z-loss, Expert-Choice, ReLU gating, and entropy regularization) across twelve configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision restructures the routing decision (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe} and Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree} eliminate the external router; ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe} replaces softmax+load-balance with eigenbasis-score routing) reaches related conclusions about restructuring the routing decision; in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} argues MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
235	
236	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics; FAN~\citep{ye2024fan} extends instance normalization beyond $(\mu,\sigma)$ to dominant Fourier components. Concurrently, \citet{berthelier2026revin} independently show that several RevIN components are redundant or detrimental, and \citet{zou2025ibnorm} argue that variance-centric normalization (BatchNorm, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) is suboptimal for representation learning because two representations with identical mean and variance can encode different amounts of task-relevant information. We identify a specific, mechanistic consequence of this principle that has not been characterized in the MoE literature: when the stripped statistics carry the \emph{routing} signal in a downstream MoE adapter, instance normalization causes the router to collapse to a single expert under unfreezing, a failure mode distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE rescue literature. Section~\ref{sec:method} formalizes this diagnosis and presents the minimal architectural fix it implies.}
237	
238	\section{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)}
239	\label{sec:method}
240

368	\label{sec:rrmoa}
369	
370	An \emph{adapter} $g_\phi: \mathbb{R}^{B \times T \times d} \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^{B \times h}$ maps a pretrained backbone's hidden states (batch $B$, sequence length $T$, hidden dim $d$) to forecasts of horizon $h$, with $n_g \leq 500{,}000$ trainable parameters. (We use lowercase $h$ for forecast horizon to disambiguate from $\mathbf{H}_\mathrm{FM}$ below, the backbone hidden state.) The goal of per-window routing is to select, for each input window, the subset of expert adapters best suited to that window's characteristics, so that a quiet baseline and a volatile spike are handled by different adapter topologies rather than forced through the same head. As shown in the introduction, this fails when the router reads normalized hidden states, because RevIN removes the per-window statistics that distinguish input regimes. \textbf{RR-MoA} avoids this by splitting the information into two streams: the frozen TSFM produces deep embeddings $\mathbf{H}$ for the experts, while a separate lightweight gate reads the \textit{raw, pre-normalization} input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ to decide which experts to activate:

462	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why does standard MoE routing fail?} To understand the source of RR-MoA's advantage, we examine AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} adapted to a learned hidden-state softmax router, an established recipe in MoE-adapter and sparsely-gated MoE work (e.g.,~\citealp{wang2022adamix,fedus2022switch}; Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}). Table~\ref{tab:adamix} shows that AdaMix collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy reaches $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations; the remaining 5 stay below $0.55$ (vs.\ max ${\approx}1.61$) with high cross-seed variance ($\sigma$ up to $0.71$), indicating stochastic collapse rather than stable partial routing. The green rows reveal the cause: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$, proving that normalization is the causal mechanism (on the ETTh1 last-4 setting we tested, BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). The causal contrast is sharpest when comparing backbones directly (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL (which lacks RevIN) stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps, opposite trajectories from the same code.}
463

467	\vspace{-4pt}
468	\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}40$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~The dominant expert (Attn) retains a gradient of ${\sim}10^{-2}$ throughout training, while the other four experts crash to the numerical noise floor (${<}10^{-3}$) by step 30 and never recover. Light shading is the per-step range across the four starved experts; the solid blue line is their mean. Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) inverts the trajectory.}
469	\label{fig:trajectory}
470	\end{figure}
471	\vspace{-4pt}
472	
473	\begin{figure}[h]

475	\includegraphics[width=0.62\columnwidth]{figures/causal_contrast.pdf}
476	\vspace{-6pt}
477	\caption{\textbf{Causal contrast: MOMENT vs Timer-XL} (ETTh1, last-4 unfreezing). Solid: unfrozen; MOMENT entropy collapses $0.51{\to}0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps; Timer-XL holds ${\approx}1.60$ for $400$ steps. Dashed: frozen controls. Dotted: $\log K{=}1.609$ ceiling. Same code, same data, only normalization differs.}

480	
481	In contrast, \textbf{RR-MoA} maintains healthy routing entropy ($1.0$--$1.57$) and wins \textbf{54/54} configurations, with MSE improvements of $26\%$ to $79\%$ over the best fixed adapter. Notably, on Traffic ($R{=}0.14$, where the stripped statistics carry little information), RR-MoA does not improve over fixed adapters, consistent with Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}'s prediction that low $R$ implies little benefit (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}).
482	
483	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} The trajectory and causal-contrast figures formalize into a sharp prediction: if normalization-driven gradient co-adaptation is the collapse mechanism, then within a fixed MoE architecture, frozen should match or beat unfrozen. Empirically, frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on all 6 LTSF datasets (Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}; the three-dataset subset in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} is consistent). The two-factor headline gap (frozen+MoE vs.\ unfrozen+single-head) decomposes into:
484	\emph{(M1) MoE architectural advantage (dominant, $26$--$79\%$):} the best fixed adapter loses to RR-MoA \emph{at the same freeze level} (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}), isolating M1 from M2.
485	\emph{(M2) Within-architecture freeze effect (smaller, $\leq 13\%$, non-unidirectional):} within RR-MoA itself, frozen is best on $4/6$ datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two; SR-MoA independently replicates the $4/6$ pattern (Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} attributes M2 to gradient co-adaptation: the Router-Detached Gradient Flow ablation (unfreezes backbone but cuts routing-weighted feedback) degrades MSE by $+35$--$100\%$ on three datasets (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}), and disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers full-FT by $54$--$61\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}), pinning the mechanism on normalization rather than optimization. A $90$-config hyperparameter tune (LR $10^{-5}$--$10^{-6}$, $100$ epochs, cosine + warmup, layerwise decay, batch up to $512$) still loses by $51$--$71\%$, consistent with optimization not being the bottleneck within the sweep we ran.}
486	
487	\begin{proposition}[Gradient Co-Adaptation Drives Entropy Collapse]
488	\label{prop:frozen}
489	Consider a $2$-expert MoE with backbone $\mathbf{h}{=}A\mathbf{x}$, router $p{=}\sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top\mathbf{h})$, loss $\ell{=}p\,\ell_1{+}(1{-}p)\,\ell_2$. Under gradient descent on $(A,\mathbf{w})$: \textup{(i)} the dominant expert's loss decreases faster ($\dot{\ell}_k \propto {-}p_k\|\nabla_\mathbf{h}\ell_k\|^2$), and $\dot{p}$ amplifies whichever expert currently has lower loss, creating self-reinforcing collapse toward $p{\in}\{0,1\}$; \textup{(ii)} freezing $A$ removes the co-adaptation feedback through the backbone (full non-collapse additionally requires $(\ell_1{-}\ell_2)$ to flip sign across input regimes, i.e., expert specialization, which is satisfied by the topologically distinct RR-MoA pool). Proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}.
490	\end{proposition}
491	
492	{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms it in the full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Independent ensembles are $37$--$46\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}): learned routing, not mere diversity, drives gains.}
493	
494	\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{wang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven new rescue configurations plus the load-balance baseline already in Table~\ref{tab:adamix} (twelve configurations total) on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ run-cells (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This is consistent with Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
495	
496	\textbf{The principle is architecture-agnostic.} The diagnosis predicts the same fix should work for any router that reads raw input. We validate this with three router-level controls. \textbf{SR-MoA} (Self-Routed MoA, Figure~\ref{fig:srmoa_arch}; following the router-restructuring direction of~\citealp{lv2025aoe,liu2026routingfree,cheng2025ermoe}) drops the external router entirely: each expert has its own sigmoid gate $\sigma_k(\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw})$. SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (per-(dataset, backbone) cell range over the $252$-run, six-backbone grid; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed} top vs.\ Tables~\ref{tab:rrmoa},~\ref{tab:rrmoa_extended} frozen rows) and replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen best on 4/6; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Gating the same SR-MoA architecture on hidden states degrades by $+87$--$768\%$ on all 6 datasets ($p{=}0.016$); on Timer-XL (no RevIN) the same raw-vs-hidden comparison yields only $+4$--$9\%$, an order-of-magnitude smaller effect on the three datasets where both backbones were tested (Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}), consistent with the effect being normalization-induced. \textbf{AdaMix-Raw} (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}) swaps AdaMix's hidden-state router for a raw-input router and recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells. \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}, Figure~\ref{fig:residual_ia_arch}) extends the principle to the expert level via a shared NLinear raw branch + gated backbone residual, closing the DLinear gap. Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls} summarizes all eight controls.
497	
498	\begin{table}[h]
499	\centering
500	\caption{\textbf{Eight causal controls.} Each rules out a competing explanation; the only mechanism consistent with all 8 is that \emph{instance normalization strips the per-window $(\mu,\sigma)$ signal the router needs}.}
501	\label{tab:causal_controls}
502	\footnotesize
503	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
504	\begin{tabular}{@{}cl >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{3.3cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{6.0cm}@{}}
505	\toprule
506	\# & \textbf{Control} & \textbf{Hypothesis (rejected)} & \textbf{Observed result} $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{verdict} \\
507	\midrule
508	C1 & RevIN ablation & Collapse is intrinsic to MoE itself, not caused by normalization & Entropy $0.000 \to 0.66$--$1.32$ when RevIN disabled (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} RevIN is \emph{necessary} for collapse \\
509	C2 & MOMENT vs Timer-XL & Architecture, not normalization, causes collapse & Opposite trajectories with identical code, only norm differs (Fig.~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} normalization is \emph{sufficient} \\
510	C3 & BatchNorm1d / GroupNorm & Collapse is unique to RevIN; other per-window normalizers are immune & All three per-window normalizers collapse identically (App.~\ref{app:norm_generalization}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} any norm that strips $(\mu,\sigma)$ triggers collapse \\
511	C4 & 5-family rescue sweep & Optimization problem (load-balance, z-loss, ReLU, expert-choice) & 0/12 configs recover; best is $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} not in the optimizer \\
512	C5 & AdaMix with raw router & Router architecture is the bottleneck & Same router, swap input only: entropy $\to 1.41$--$1.57$; MSE $-41$--$88\%$ (App.~\ref{app:adamix_details}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} the \emph{input} matters, not the router \\
513	C6 & SR-MoA raw vs hidden routing & The fix only works for RR-MoA's specific Conv1d router design & Different router (per-expert sigmoid); hidden-routed $+87$--$768\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} raw-input principle generalizes \\
514	C7 & Vision ViT + InstanceNorm & Collapse only happens in time series, not other modalities & ResNet/ViT MoE on CIFAR-10 collapses identically (App.~\ref{app:vision_moe}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} mechanism is modality-general \\
515	C8 & $(\mu,\sigma)$ re-injection & Just appending stripped stats back fixes it & Re-injected hidden states still $+42$--$239\%$ worse, 11/18 still collapsed (App.~\ref{app:reinjection}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} only \emph{raw} pre-normalization input restores routing \\
516	\bottomrule
517	\end{tabular}
518	\end{table}
519	
520	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Two distinct gains.} \emph{(Q1)} the best fixed adapter on the same RevIN-normalized hidden states still loses to RR-MoA by $26$--$79\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}): residual shape heterogeneity survives normalization and rewards diverse experts; \emph{(Q2)} the signal-ratio $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ measures \emph{dispatch}-signal localization in the stripped statistics, explaining why dense routing also benefits from raw access, and why Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly does not improve. The Pure Raw-MLP MoE ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}) shows the dataset-conditional choice between ``MoE with raw router'' and ``ensemble with raw input''.}
521	\vspace{-0.3em}
522

549	
550	RR-MoA outperforms every baseline ($p{<}0.001$). The independent ensemble (same experts, no router) is $37$--$46\%$ worse, confirming learned routing adds value beyond diversity.
551	
552	\textbf{Closing the DLinear gap.} DLinear (49K params, from scratch) outperforms frozen adapters in absolute MSE (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, grey rows): k-NN/Ridge diagnostics indicate this reflects information loss in the normalization--encoding pipeline (Appendix~\ref{app:diagnostic}). Restoring raw access \emph{at the expert level} closes it: \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}} matches or beats DLinear on $6/6$ datasets at $H{=}96$ and $107/123$ cells across six backbones (Appendix~\ref{app:gate_pathology}); a self-routed SR-RIA\textsuperscript{+} hits $6/6$ (Appendix~\ref{app:sr_ria}) and a TSFM-free Pure Raw-MLP MoE control localizes the TSFM's contribution (Appendix~\ref{app:raw_mlp_moe}).
553	
554	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Rawness vs.\ bypass and dose-response.} Replacing the router's raw input with RevIN-normalized input degrades MSE by $60$--$88\%$ while entropy stays high (Table~\ref{tab:router_input}): the router relies on the \emph{content} of the stripped statistics, not the bypass path. A dose-response interpolation $\mathbf{x}_\alpha = (1{-}\alpha)\mathbf{x}_\text{raw} + \alpha\,\text{RevIN}(\mathbf{x}_\text{raw})$ shows a phase transition at $\alpha{\to}1$ (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}; $150$ runs; heuristic SNR-onset $\alpha^*{\approx}0.40$, Appendix~\ref{app:prop2_depth}); a temporal-shuffle ablation confirms routing depends on $(M,\Sigma)$, not temporal ordering (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).}
555	
556	
557	\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing.} Each window decomposes into mean $M_i$, scale $\Sigma_i$, shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$; RevIN keeps $\mathbf{S}_i$ and discards $(M_i,\Sigma_i)$. The chain rule quantifies how much routing signal lives in the discarded part.
558	
559	\begin{observation}[Routing Information Loss Under Instance Normalization]
560	\label{thm:mi_decomp}
561	Let $X$ be a random input window, $E \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, $S = (X - M)/\Sigma$ the RevIN-normalized shape. \emph{(i) Exact decomposition:} $I(X;E) - I(S;E) = I(M,\Sigma;E\mid S) \geq 0$. \emph{(ii) Quantitative bound (no independence assumption):} $I(X;E) - I(S;E) \geq I(M,\Sigma;E) - I(M,\Sigma;S)$, with $\varepsilon := I(M,\Sigma;S) \geq 0$ capturing residual leakage of stripped statistics through the shape; equality iff $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid E$.
562	\end{observation}
563	
564	When routing signal lives entirely in the stripped statistics ($(M,\Sigma)\perp\!\!\!\perp S$; Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}), $I(X;E){-}I(S;E){=}H(E)$: RevIN destroys the entire routing distribution. The signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M)+\mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)]/\overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ is a tractable monotone proxy; the bound is empirically tight (Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}) and learnable $\alpha{<}0.5$ on $30/30$ runs (Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}). $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts raw-routing benefit at Spearman $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), whereas $H(E\mid S)$ alone does not ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$); Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) is flagged a boundary, Solar ($R{=}0.06$) the one outlier (Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}).
565	
566	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins on every cell for instance-normalized backbones (MOMENT-small/large $26$--$79\%/32$--$75\%$, Moirai $12$--$29\%$, Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} $37$--$90\%$); LayerNorm-only controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}) show no collapse. Top-$2$, imputation $7/8$, $24/24$ horizons, and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} ($6{\times}5{\times}4$) confirm robustness (Tables~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}--\ref{tab:horizon}; Appendices~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation},~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}
567	
568	\vspace{-0.6em}
569	\section{Conclusion and Limitations}
570	\vspace{-0.6em}
571	
572	MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization handling distribution shift also strips per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ flags applicable settings (vision replication, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). \textbf{Limitations:} instance-level only; monotone $R(\mathcal{D})$; forecasting + imputation.\label{page:end_main}
573	\bibliographystyle{plainnat}
574	\begin{thebibliography}{20}
```

66:Standard TSFM adapters apply a lightweight regression head on encoder outputs through the same head for every window (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}a) -- flatten-then-project for MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment} and PatchTST~\citep{nie2023patchtst}, an autoregressive residual block for TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}. Real time series mix heterogeneous regimes (quiet baselines, seasonal excursions, abrupt shape changes), and instance normalization handles only the scale-and-offset component of that heterogeneity; residual \emph{shape} heterogeneity (periodicity, trends, level shifts, change points) is encoded in the hidden states across layers, as recent probing analyses of MOMENT and related TSFMs have reported~\citep{wilinski2025tsfm_representations, pandey2025tsfm_semantics}. This residual heterogeneity motivates a per-window mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable one. Keeping the backbone frozen while training only the adapter is also what makes deployment practical: a shared TSFM in GPU memory can serve many tenants through lightweight per-task adapters hot-swapped from host RAM, an arrangement that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot match (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}).
68:This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the AdaMix-style adapter pool~\citep{wang2022adamix} is combined with a learned hidden-state softmax router and Switch-style load balancing~\citep{fedus2022switch}, and applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes (formalized in Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}; signal-ratio diagnostic in Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}), leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}).
224:\label{fig:problem_overview}
364:\label{fig:framework}
462:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Why does standard MoE routing fail?} To understand the source of RR-MoA's advantage, we examine AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} adapted to a learned hidden-state softmax router, an established recipe in MoE-adapter and sparsely-gated MoE work (e.g.,~\citealp{wang2022adamix,fedus2022switch}; Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}). Table~\ref{tab:adamix} shows that AdaMix collapses to a single expert whenever backbone layers are unfrozen: entropy reaches $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations; the remaining 5 stay below $0.55$ (vs.\ max ${\approx}1.61$) with high cross-seed variance ($\sigma$ up to $0.71$), indicating stochastic collapse rather than stable partial routing. The green rows reveal the cause: disabling RevIN inside MOMENT recovers entropy from $0.000$ to $0.66$--$1.32$, proving that normalization is the causal mechanism (on the ETTh1 last-4 setting we tested, BatchNorm1d and GroupNorm produce the same collapse; Appendix~\ref{app:norm_generalization}). The causal contrast is sharpest when comparing backbones directly (Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}): under identical last-4 unfreezing on identical data, MOMENT entropy crashes to $0.000$ in ${\sim}40$ steps while Timer-XL (which lacks RevIN) stays at $1.60$ for all $400$ steps, opposite trajectories from the same code.}
468:\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}40$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~The dominant expert (Attn) retains a gradient of ${\sim}10^{-2}$ throughout training, while the other four experts crash to the numerical noise floor (${<}10^{-3}$) by step 30 and never recover. Light shading is the per-step range across the four starved experts; the solid blue line is their mean. Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) inverts the trajectory.}
469:\label{fig:trajectory}
478:\label{fig:causal_contrast}
492:{\looseness=-1 \textit{Remark.} Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen} covers linear $A\mathbf{x}$; Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}b confirms it in the full 8-block Transformer within ${\sim}50$ steps. Independent ensembles are $37$--$46\%$ worse (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}): learned routing, not mere diversity, drives gains.}
509:C2 & MOMENT vs Timer-XL & Architecture, not normalization, causes collapse & Opposite trajectories with identical code, only norm differs (Fig.~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) {\small$\Rightarrow$} normalization is \emph{sufficient} \\
1165:This corollary identifies raw routing as the \emph{minimal causal fix}: it intervenes on the precise term in $\partial\ell/\partial A$ that drives the collapse loop, leaving all other components of the model untouched. The empirical validation is in Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory} (RR-MoA's gradient norms remain balanced across experts even when blocks are unfrozen) and Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} (frozen-vs-unfrozen RR-MoA both maintain healthy entropy, in contrast to AdaMix where unfreezing collapses entropy to $0.000$).
1397:\caption{\textbf{SR-MoA architecture (Self-Routed Mixture of Adapters).} Each of $K{=}5$ experts has its own sigmoid gate $\sigma_k$ reading $\mathbf{X}_\mathrm{raw}$ directly, with no external router. The frozen TSFM produces $\mathbf{H}$, and each expert's contribution is scaled by its self-gate. SR-MoA shares only the \emph{raw-input} principle with RR-MoA (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}): the routing architecture is otherwise entirely different. SR-MoA outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets, providing architecture-agnostic validation that the principle, not the specific router, drives the gains.}

\subsection{AdaMix Implementation Details}
\label{app:adamix_details}

Our AdaMix baseline follows \citet{wang2022adamix} with $K{=}5$ expert adapter heads identical to RR-MoA's canonical pool (mean, last, max, attention, conv1d). The router is a single linear layer $\mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^K$ operating on the mean-pooled hidden state, with softmax gating. We use a load-balancing auxiliary loss with coefficient $\lambda_\text{bal}{=}0.01$, matching AdaMix's original formulation. No stochastic routing, capacity factors, or noise injection are applied, since these standard MoE safeguards do not address the normalization-specific signal loss we identify: when RevIN strips the per-window statistics that would distinguish samples, the router receives near-identical inputs regardless of stabilization strategy. This is confirmed empirically: load-balancing loss does not prevent collapse under unfreezing (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}, entropy $= 0.000$ with $\lambda_\text{bal}{=}0.01$), while disabling RevIN recovers entropy to $0.66$--$1.32$ without any routing regularization.

\textbf{Architecture-agnostic validation (AdaMix-Raw).} To confirm the diagnosis generalizes beyond RR-MoA, we replace AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input Conv1d router (identical architecture to RR-MoA's) while keeping AdaMix's expert heads and training loop unchanged. Table~\ref{tab:adamix_raw} shows results across 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds (36 runs).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering

\label{tab:adamix_raw}

\end{proof}

\textbf{Corollary (Raw routing decouples the router from $A$).}
\label{cor:raw_routing}
\emph{If the router reads the raw input $\mathbf{x}_\mathrm{raw}$ rather than the hidden state $\mathbf{h} = A\mathbf{x}$, then $\partial p / \partial A \equiv 0$, the loss-coupling term in $\partial\ell/\partial A$ vanishes identically, and the co-adaptation feedback loop of Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}(i) is broken \emph{by construction} regardless of whether $A$ is frozen.}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary]
With router input $\mathbf{x}_\mathrm{raw}$ (independent of $A$), $p = \sigma(\mathbf{w}^\top G_\psi(\mathbf{x}_\mathrm{raw}))$ is a function of $\mathbf{x}_\mathrm{raw}$ and $(\mathbf{w}, \psi)$ only. Hence $\partial p / \partial A = 0$, and the third term $(\ell_1 - \ell_2)\,p(1{-}p)\,\mathbf{w}\mathbf{x}^\top$ in $\partial\ell/\partial A$ disappears: changes in $A$ no longer feed back into the routing decision through the gradient. The dominant-expert runaway in Part~(i) required this term; without it, even when $A$ is unfrozen, the router's update is decoupled from the backbone's update, eliminating the co-adaptation mechanism.
\end{proof}

This corollary identifies raw routing as the \emph{minimal causal fix}: it intervenes on the precise term in $\partial\ell/\partial A$ that drives the collapse loop, leaving all other components of the model untouched. The empirical validation is in Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory} (RR-MoA's gradient norms remain balanced across experts even when blocks are unfrozen) and Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa} (frozen-vs-unfrozen RR-MoA both maintain healthy entropy, in contrast to AdaMix where unfreezing collapses entropy to $0.000$).

\section{LoRA Sweep (Full Results)}
\label{app:lora_sweep}

We sweep LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora} across three axes to ensure the comparison in Table~\ref{tab:baselines} is not cherry-picked: rank $r{\in}\{8,16,32\}$, target projections ${\in}\{q{+}v,\; q{+}k{+}v{+}o\}$, and forecast head ${\in}\{$linear, 2-layer MLP$\}$, for 12 configurations $\times$ 3 seeds $=$ 36 runs per dataset (108 runs total across the 3 datasets). All runs use a strictly frozen backbone (matching Table~\ref{tab:rrmoa}'s primary setting). The trio \{ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather\} covers an hourly ETT dataset, a 15-min ETT dataset, and a multivariate dataset with $21$ channels; we restrict the LoRA sweep to this trio to keep total compute bounded ($\sim 3.6$ GPU-hours), since the broader 6-dataset grid is already covered for every other baseline in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}.

\textbf{LoRA hyperparameters.} Following the original LoRA recipe, we set $\alpha = 2r$ at every rank, layer placement = all encoder blocks, no LoRA dropout, and train with the same optimizer used everywhere else in the paper (Adam, lr$=10^{-3}$, batch 128, 15 epochs, bfloat16 AMP). Both heads pool the encoder output by mean over the time axis; the linear head is $d_\text{model}\to H$, and the 2-layer MLP head is $d_\text{model}\to 128\to H$ with GELU and dropout $0.1$ between the two linear layers. The bolded ``best per dataset'' row is the configuration with the lowest mean MSE across the 3 seeds (ties broken by lower std).

\begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{Full LoRA sweep (strictly frozen backbone, 3 seeds, test MSE mean$\pm$std). Bold rows are the best-per-dataset configuration used in Table~\ref{tab:baselines}. No LoRA variant beats the RR-MoA (Top-2) result on any dataset.}
\label{tab:lora_sweep}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llllc@{}}

  "old_string": "\\vspace{-0.6em}\n\\section{Conclusion and Limitations}\n\\vspace{-0.6em}\n\nMoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization handling distribution shift also strips per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\\mathcal{D})$ flags applicable settings (vision replication, Appendix~\\ref{app:vision_moe}). \\textbf{Limitations:} instance-level only; monotone $R(\\mathcal{D})$; forecasting + imputation.\\label{page:end_main}",
  "new_string": "\\vspace{-0.4em}\n\\section{Conclusion and Limitations}\n\\vspace{-0.3em}\n\n\\textbf{Diagnosis.} We identified \\emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: MoE adapters on instance-normalized time series foundation models collapse to a single expert because the same per-window normalization that handles distribution shift also strips the $(\\mu,\\sigma)$ statistics the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Observation~\\ref{thm:mi_decomp} formalizes the loss as a mutual-information gap; the signal-ratio proxy $R(\\mathcal{D})$ predicts where it bites at Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$); and eight causal controls (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_controls}) jointly rule out optimization, geometry, and routing-paradigm explanations.\n\n\\textbf{Three fixes share one ingredient.} Three architecturally distinct interventions, all giving the router or the experts access to the raw pre-normalization input, confirm the diagnosis: RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against the best fixed adapter ($26$--$79\\%$ MSE); SR-MoA drops the external router entirely and outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\\%$; Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+} extends the principle to the expert level and closes the long-standing gap to DLinear on $107/123$ cross-backbone cells. The mechanism is modality-general (replicated on ResNet/ViT MoE with InstanceNorm on CIFAR-10, Appendix~\\ref{app:vision_moe}) and absent from LayerNorm-only backbones (Timer-XL, raw vs.\\ hidden routing differ by only $4$--$9\\%$, $15$--$100{\\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT), so the normalization \\emph{type}, not the modality or the architecture, is the relevant axis.\n\n\\textbf{Limitations.} The diagnosis is specific to per-window normalizers that strip $(\\mu,\\sigma)$ from the input (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm, vision InstanceNorm); LayerNorm- and RMSNorm-only backbones exhibit no collapse and no fix is needed. $R(\\mathcal{D})$ is a tractable monotone proxy rather than a sharp decision threshold: it correctly flags Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) as a boundary case, but predicting which intermediate $R$ values benefit at which freeze level remains open. Empirical evaluation covers forecasting and imputation; classification and anomaly detection remain to be tested. The fix presumes raw pre-normalization input is accessible at routing time, which holds for adapter-style finetuning of TSFMs but not for purely API-served foundation models. Our expert pool is also a fixed set of five hand-coded topologies; whether learned expert pools amplify or attenuate the gain is open.\n\n\\textbf{Outlook.} The signal-ratio diagnostic is portable: any future MoE-adapter design on a normalized backbone can compute $R(\\mathcal{D})$ on the target distribution before training to predict whether raw routing is required. Surfacing the upstream architectural cause of routing collapse, rather than treating it as an optimization symptom to be patched with load balancing or z-loss, widens the design space of MoE adapters that have so far been blocked by ineffective rescue mechanisms.\\label{page:end_main}"
}

306   5   Conclusion and Limitations
307   Diagnosis. We identified normalization-induced routing collapse: MoE adapters on instance-
308   normalized time series foundation models collapse to a single expert because the same per-window
309   normalization that handles distribution shift also strips the (µ, σ) statistics the router needs to distin-
310   guish input regimes. Observation 1 formalizes the loss as a mutual-information gap; the signal-ratio
311   proxy R(D) predicts where it bites at Spearman ρ=−0.88 (p<0.002); and eight causal controls
312   (Table 3) jointly rule out optimization, geometry, and routing-paradigm explanations.
313   Three fixes share one ingredient. Three architecturally distinct interventions, all giving the router or
314   the experts access to the raw pre-normalization input, confirm the diagnosis: RR-MoA wins 54/54
315   cells against the best fixed adapter (26–79% MSE); SR-MoA drops the external router entirely and
316   outperforms RR-MoA by 13–42%; Residual-IA+ extends the principle to the expert level and closes
317   the long-standing gap to DLinear on 107/123 cross-backbone cells. The mechanism is modality-
318   general (replicated on ResNet/ViT MoE with InstanceNorm on CIFAR-10, Appendix H.1) and absent
319   from LayerNorm-only backbones (Timer-XL, raw vs. hidden routing differ by only 4–9%, 15–100×
320   smaller than on MOMENT), so the normalization type, not the modality or the architecture, is the
321   relevant axis.
322   Limitations. The diagnosis is specific to per-window normalizers that strip (µ, σ) from the input
323   (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm, vision InstanceNorm); LayerNorm- and RMSNorm-only back-
324   bones exhibit no collapse and no fix is needed. R(D) is a tractable monotone proxy rather than a
325   sharp decision threshold: it correctly flags Traffic (R=0.14) as a boundary case, but predicting which


                                                          9
326   intermediate R values benefit at which freeze level remains open. Empirical evaluation covers fore-
327   casting and imputation; classification and anomaly detection remain to be tested. The fix presumes
328   raw pre-normalization input is accessible at routing time, which holds for adapter-style finetuning of
329   TSFMs but not for purely API-served foundation models. Our expert pool is also a fixed set of five
330   hand-coded topologies; whether learned expert pools amplify or attenuate the gain is open.
331   Outlook. The signal-ratio diagnostic is portable: any future MoE-adapter design on a normalized
332   backbone can compute R(D) on the target distribution before training to predict whether raw routing
333   is required. Surfacing the upstream architectural cause of routing collapse, rather than treating it as
334   an optimization symptom to be patched with load balancing or z-loss, widens the design space of
335   MoE adapters that have so far been blocked by ineffective rescue mechanisms.

336   References

  "old_string": "\\vspace{-0.4em}\n\\section{Conclusion and Limitations}\n\\vspace{-0.3em}\n\n\\textbf{Diagnosis.} We identified \\emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: MoE adapters on instance-normalized time series foundation models collapse to a single expert because the same per-window normalization that handles distribution shift also strips the $(\\mu,\\sigma)$ statistics the router needs to distinguish input regimes. Observation~\\ref{thm:mi_decomp} formalizes the loss as a mutual-information gap; the signal-ratio proxy $R(\\mathcal{D})$ predicts where it bites at Spearman $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$); and eight causal controls (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_controls}) jointly rule out optimization, geometry, and routing-paradigm explanations.\n\n\\textbf{Three fixes share one ingredient.} Three architecturally distinct interventions, all giving the router or the experts access to the raw pre-normalization input, confirm the diagnosis: RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells against the best fixed adapter ($26$--$79\\%$ MSE); SR-MoA drops the external router entirely and outperforms RR-MoA by $13$--$42\\%$; Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+} extends the principle to the expert level and closes the long-standing gap to DLinear on $107/123$ cross-backbone cells. The mechanism is modality-general (replicated on ResNet/ViT MoE with InstanceNorm on CIFAR-10, Appendix~\\ref{app:vision_moe}) and absent from LayerNorm-only backbones (Timer-XL, raw vs.\\ hidden routing differ by only $4$--$9\\%$, $15$--$100{\\times}$ smaller than on MOMENT), so the normalization \\emph{type}, not the modality or the architecture, is the relevant axis.\n\n\\textbf{Limitations.} The diagnosis is specific to per-window normalizers that strip $(\\mu,\\sigma)$ from the input (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm, vision InstanceNorm); LayerNorm- and RMSNorm-only backbones exhibit no collapse and no fix is needed. $R(\\mathcal{D})$ is a tractable monotone proxy rather than a sharp decision threshold: it correctly flags Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) as a boundary case, but predicting which intermediate $R$ values benefit at which freeze level remains open. Empirical evaluation covers forecasting and imputation; classification and anomaly detection remain to be tested. The fix presumes raw pre-normalization input is accessible at routing time, which holds for adapter-style finetuning of TSFMs but not for purely API-served foundation models. Our expert pool is also a fixed set of five hand-coded topologies; whether learned expert pools amplify or attenuate the gain is open.\n\n\\textbf{Outlook.} The signal-ratio diagnostic is portable: any future MoE-adapter design on a normalized backbone can compute $R(\\mathcal{D})$ on the target distribution before training to predict whether raw routing is required. Surfacing the upstream architectural cause of routing collapse, rather than treating it as an optimization symptom to be patched with load balancing or z-loss, widens the design space of MoE adapters that have so far been blocked by ineffective rescue mechanisms.\\label{page:end_main}",
  "new_string": "\\vspace{-0.5em}\n\\section{Conclusion and Limitations}\n\\vspace{-0.4em}\n\n\\textbf{Diagnosis and fix.} We identified \\emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs collapse to a single expert because the same per-window normalization handling distribution shift also strips the $(\\mu,\\sigma)$ statistics the router needs. Observation~\\ref{thm:mi_decomp} formalizes the loss as a mutual-information gap, the signal-ratio proxy $R(\\mathcal{D})$ predicts it at $\\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$), and eight causal controls (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_controls}) jointly rule out optimization, geometry, and routing-paradigm explanations. Three architecturally distinct interventions confirm the diagnosis by giving the router or the experts raw pre-normalization access: RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells ($26$--$79\\%$); SR-MoA drops the external router entirely and beats RR-MoA by $13$--$42\\%$; Residual-IA\\textsuperscript{+} extends the fix to the expert level and closes the DLinear gap on $107/123$ cross-backbone cells. The mechanism replicates on ResNet/ViT MoE with InstanceNorm (Appendix~\\ref{app:vision_moe}) and is absent from LayerNorm-only Timer-XL ($4$--$9\\%$, $15$--$100{\\times}$ smaller), so normalization \\emph{type}, not modality or backbone, is the relevant axis.\n\n\\textbf{Limitations.} The diagnosis is specific to per-window normalizers that strip $(\\mu,\\sigma)$ (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm, InstanceNorm); LayerNorm- and RMSNorm-only backbones need no fix. $R(\\mathcal{D})$ is a monotone proxy, not a sharp threshold: it flags Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly, but which intermediate $R$ benefits at which freeze level is open. Evaluation covers forecasting and imputation; classification and anomaly detection remain. The fix requires raw input at routing time, which holds for adapter-style finetuning but not for purely API-served foundation models, and our expert pool is fixed at five hand-coded topologies; learned pools are an open direction.\n\n\\textbf{Outlook.} $R(\\mathcal{D})$ is portable: any MoE-adapter design on a normalized backbone can compute it on the target distribution before training. Surfacing the upstream architectural cause of routing collapse, rather than patching it with load balancing or z-loss downstream, widens the MoE-adapter design space that ineffective rescue mechanisms have so far blocked.\\label{page:end_main}"
}

306   5   Conclusion and Limitations
307   Diagnosis and fix. We identified normalization-induced routing collapse: MoE adapters on instance-
308   normalized TSFMs collapse to a single expert because the same per-window normalization handling
309   distribution shift also strips the (µ, σ) statistics the router needs. Observation 1 formalizes the loss as
310   a mutual-information gap, the signal-ratio proxy R(D) predicts it at ρ=−0.88 (p<0.002), and eight
311   causal controls (Table 3) jointly rule out optimization, geometry, and routing-paradigm explanations.
312   Three architecturally distinct interventions confirm the diagnosis by giving the router or the experts
313   raw pre-normalization access: RR-MoA wins 54/54 cells (26–79%); SR-MoA drops the external
314   router entirely and beats RR-MoA by 13–42%; Residual-IA+ extends the fix to the expert level and
315   closes the DLinear gap on 107/123 cross-backbone cells. The mechanism replicates on ResNet/ViT
316   MoE with InstanceNorm (Appendix H.1) and is absent from LayerNorm-only Timer-XL (4–9%,
317   15–100× smaller), so normalization type, not modality or backbone, is the relevant axis.
318   Limitations. The diagnosis is specific to per-window normalizers that strip (µ, σ) (RevIN, Batch-
319   Norm1d, GroupNorm, InstanceNorm); LayerNorm- and RMSNorm-only backbones need no fix.
320   R(D) is a monotone proxy, not a sharp threshold: it flags Traffic (R=0.14) correctly, but which
321   intermediate R benefits at which freeze level is open. Evaluation covers forecasting and imputation;
322   classification and anomaly detection remain. The fix requires raw input at routing time, which holds
323   for adapter-style finetuning but not for purely API-served foundation models, and our expert pool is
324   fixed at five hand-coded topologies; learned pools are an open direction.


                                                          9
===PAGE 10===
325   Outlook. R(D) is portable: any MoE-adapter design on a normalized backbone can compute it on
326   the target distribution before training. Surfacing the upstream architectural cause of routing collapse,
327   rather than patching it with load balancing or z-loss downstream, widens the MoE-adapter design
328   space that ineffective rescue mechanisms have so far blocked.

329   References

**What changed**

- **Moved `fig:trajectory`** from main body (between `tab:adamix` and `fig:causal_contrast`) into Appendix §G.2 AdaMix Implementation Details. It became `Figure G.1`; all 4 cross-references (intro, main body Remark, corollary, and the new pointer in `fig:causal_contrast`'s caption) resolve correctly. main.tex:1612 (new home) / main.tex:464-471 (removed).
- **Slight shrink** on `fig:causal_contrast`: `0.62` → `0.60` columnwidth, with a one-clause pointer added to its caption directing readers to Figure G.1 for the gradient-norm panel.
- **Expanded Conclusion** from 1 dense sentence (~85 words) to 2 substantive paragraphs (~280 words) at main.tex:570-575:
  - **Diagnosis and fix**: names the phenomenon, formalizes via Observation 1 + R(D), cites the 8 causal controls, and summarizes the three architectural interventions (RR-MoA 54/54, SR-MoA +13-42%, Residual-IA⁺ 107/123) with the modality + Timer-XL negative-control framing.
  - **Limitations and outlook**: scopes the diagnosis to per-window normalizers (RevIN/BN/GN/IN); flags R(D) as a monotone proxy rather than a sharp threshold; calls out classification/anomaly detection as untested; notes the raw-input requirement (rules out API-served models); flags the fixed 5-expert pool; closes with R(D) as a portable pre-training diagnostic.

Net effect: page-9 compliance preserved, conclusion is now proportional to the rest of §1-§4 instead of being squeezed to 4 lines.

 {\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone, task, horizon.}\label{sec:cross_backbone}\label{sec:topk} RR-MoA wins on every cell for instance-normalized backbones (MOMENT-small/large $26$--$79\%/32$--$75\%$, Moirai $12$--$29\%$, Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} $37$--$90\%$); LayerNorm-only controls (Chronos, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}) show no collapse. Top-$2$, imputation $7/8$, $24/24$ horizons, and Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} ($6{\times}5{\times}4$) confirm robustness (Tables~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}--\ref{tab:horizon}; Appendices~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation},~\ref{app:gate_pathology}).}
 
-\vspace{-0.6em}
+\vspace{-0.5em}
 \section{Conclusion and Limitations}
-\vspace{-0.6em}
+\vspace{-0.4em}
 
-MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs fail because the normalization handling distribution shift also strips per-window statistics the router needs; raw pre-normalization routing fixes it, motivates Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, and $R(\mathcal{D})$ flags applicable settings (vision replication, Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). \textbf{Limitations:} instance-level only; monotone $R(\mathcal{D})$; forecasting + imputation.\label{page:end_main}
+\textbf{Diagnosis and fix.} We identified \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: MoE adapters on instance-normalized TSFMs collapse to a single expert because the same per-window normalization handling distribution shift also strips the $(\mu,\sigma)$ statistics the router needs. Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} formalizes the loss as a mutual-information gap, the signal-ratio proxy $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts it at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$), and eight causal controls (Table~\ref{tab:causal_controls}) jointly rule out optimization, geometry, and routing-paradigm explanations. Three architecturally distinct interventions confirm the diagnosis by giving the router or the experts raw pre-normalization access: RR-MoA wins $54/54$ cells ($26$--$79\%$); SR-MoA drops the external router entirely and beats RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$; Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+} extends the fix to the expert level and closes the DLinear gap on $107/123$ cross-backbone cells. The mechanism replicates on ResNet/ViT MoE with InstanceNorm (Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}) and is absent from LayerNorm-only Timer-XL ($4$--$9\%$, $15$--$100{\times}$ smaller), so normalization \emph{type}, not modality or backbone, is the relevant axis.
+
+\textbf{Limitations and outlook.} The diagnosis is specific to per-window normalizers that strip $(\mu,\sigma)$ (RevIN, BatchNorm1d, GroupNorm, InstanceNorm); LayerNorm- and RMSNorm-only backbones need no fix. $R(\mathcal{D})$ is a monotone proxy, not a sharp threshold: it flags Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) correctly, but which intermediate $R$ benefits at which freeze level is open. Evaluation covers forecasting and imputation; classification and anomaly detection remain. The fix needs raw input at routing time, available in adapter-style finetuning but not in purely API-served models; our pool is also fixed at five hand-coded experts (learned pools are open). $R(\mathcal{D})$ itself is portable: any MoE-adapter on a normalized backbone can compute it pre-training, and surfacing this \emph{upstream architectural} cause widens a design space that downstream rescues have so far blocked.\label{page:end_main}
 \bibliographystyle{plainnat}
 \begin{thebibliography}{20}
 
@@ -1610,6 +1603,14 @@ Expert-choice ($c{=}2$)                      & $1.590$ ($-3.9\%$) & $0.17$ & \te
 
 Our AdaMix baseline follows \citet{wang2022adamix} with $K{=}5$ expert adapter heads identical to RR-MoA's canonical pool (mean, last, max, attention, conv1d). The router is a single linear layer $\mathbb{R}^d \to \mathbb{R}^K$ operating on the mean-pooled hidden state, with softmax gating. We use a load-balancing auxiliary loss with coefficient $\lambda_\text{bal}{=}0.01$, matching AdaMix's original formulation. No stochastic routing, capacity factors, or noise injection are applied, since these standard MoE safeguards do not address the normalization-specific signal loss we identify: when RevIN strips the per-window statistics that would distinguish samples, the router receives near-identical inputs regardless of stabilization strategy. This is confirmed empirically: load-balancing loss does not prevent collapse under unfreezing (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}, entropy $= 0.000$ with $\lambda_\text{bal}{=}0.01$), while disabling RevIN recovers entropy to $0.66$--$1.32$ without any routing regularization.
 
+\begin{figure}[h]
+\centering
+\includegraphics[width=0.92\columnwidth]{figures/adamix_trajectory.pdf}
+\vspace{-4pt}
+\caption{\textbf{Per-step routing / gradient trajectory} (AdaMix, ETTh1). (a)~Unfrozen+RevIN (red) collapses within ${\sim}40$ steps; strictly frozen control (blue) stays stable, isolating gradient co-adaptation from optimizer or data effects. (b)~The dominant expert (Attn) retains a gradient of ${\sim}10^{-2}$ throughout training, while the other four experts crash to the numerical noise floor (${<}10^{-3}$) by step 30 and never recover. Light shading is the per-step range across the four starved experts; the solid blue line is their mean. Removing RevIN (separate experiment, Figure~\ref{fig:causal_contrast}) inverts the trajectory.}
+\label{fig:trajectory}
+\end{figure}
+
 \textbf{Architecture-agnostic validation (AdaMix-Raw).} To confirm the diagnosis generalizes beyond RR-MoA, we replace AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input Conv1d router (identical architecture to RR-MoA's) while keeping AdaMix's expert heads and training loop unchanged. Table~\ref{tab:adamix_raw} shows results across 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 3 seeds (36 runs).

Based on the search results, I can provide you with a clear answer about the NeurIPS 2026 paper checklist position.

According to the official NeurIPS guidelines, all submissions must include, in this order, (1) the submitted paper, (2) optional technical appendices that support the paper with additional proofs, derivations, or results, and (3) the NeurIPS paper checklist. The checklist should therefore follow the submission's references and the (optional) supplemental material.

However, there is some flexibility on this point. The checklist can go either after or before the appendix in the PDF.

To summarize: **The recommended order is paper → references → appendix → checklist, but the checklist can alternatively be placed before the appendix if preferred.** The checklist does NOT count towards the page limit.

Based on the search results, I found information about the NeurIPS 2026 reproducibility checklist and its placement instructions:

## Placement Instructions

All submissions must be in PDF format, and in a single PDF file include, in this order, (1) the submitted paper; (2) optional technical appendices that support the paper with additional proofs, derivations, or results; (3) the NeurIPS paper checklist. More specifically, the checklist should follow the submissions's references and the (optional) supplemental material.

## Key Requirements

Papers not including the checklist will be desk rejected. Additionally, the checklist does NOT count towards the page limit.

## Checklist Purpose

The NeurIPS Paper Checklist is designed to encourage best practices for responsible machine learning research, addressing issues of reproducibility, transparency, research ethics, and societal impact. The checklist is included in the LaTeX style file.

The search results confirm that for each question in the checklist, you should answer yes, no, or n/a.

According to the handbook, submissions must include components in this specific sequence:

1. The submitted paper content
2. Paper references

**Verdict from web search.** The NeurIPS 2026 Main Track Handbook prescribes the *suggested* order as: **(1) paper body → (2) references → (3) appendices → (4) checklist**. The checklist sits at the very end, after the appendix.

Caveat: the @NeurIPSConf account confirmed in a 2024 post that "the checklist can go either after or before the appendix in the PDF" — so it is technically flexible, but the canonical 2026 ordering puts it last.

**Our paper is currently in the wrong order**: `main.tex:894-900` has references → checklist → appendix, with the appendix coming after the checklist. To match the suggested NeurIPS 2026 ordering, we should swap to references → appendix → checklist. Want me to make that swap? It is a small structural edit (move the `\input{checklist}` from line 897 to after the last appendix section, i.e., just before `\end{document}`).

- [NeurIPS 2026 Main Track Handbook](https://neurips.cc/Conferences/2026/MainTrackHandbook)

915	\paragraph{Backbone specifications.} MOMENT-small ($d{=}512$, 8 encoder blocks, internal RevIN with learnable affine); MOMENT-large ($d{=}1024$, 24 blocks, internal RevIN with learnable affine); Moirai ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, RMSNorm internally with non-learnable per-instance I/O scaling, no learnable-affine norm inside the encoder); Moirai-MoE ($d{=}384$, 6 blocks, RMSNorm internally with sparse internal experts and non-learnable per-instance I/O scaling); Chronos ($d{=}512$, 6 encoder blocks, T5 RMSNorm internally, no instance normalization); Timer-XL ($d{=}1024$, 8 blocks, LayerNorm internally, no instance normalization). Throughout, ``full fine-tuning'' means \emph{all} encoder blocks are unfrozen (8/8 for MOMENT-small, 24/24 for MOMENT-large, 6/6 for Moirai/Moirai-MoE/Chronos, 8/8 for Timer-XL).
916	
917	\paragraph{Training hyperparameters.} Adam optimizer, learning rate $10^{-3}$, MSE loss, batch size $128$, $15$ epochs. Final reported numbers are 15-epoch test MSE. Core multi-seed grids use seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45, 46\}$ ($n{=}5$); ablations use seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$ ($n{=}3$). \begin{table}[h]
\centering
\caption{\textbf{Pure Raw-MLP MoE vs.\ Dual-Stream and DLinear} (5 seeds, mean$\pm$std, H$=$96). The Raw-MLP MoE has \emph{no TSFM at all}. It matches Dual-Stream within $\pm 7\%$ on every dataset, and \emph{beats} Dual-Stream outright on $\mathbf{3}$ of $\mathbf{6}$ datasets (ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity). Mean delta to Dual-Stream is $-1.5\%$: the TSFM's contribution is dataset-dependent and negligible in the average case.}
\label{tab:raw_mlp_moe}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccrr@{}}
\toprule
Dataset & Raw-MLP MoE & Dual-Stream & DLinear & $\Delta$ vs DS & $\Delta$ vs DL \\
\midrule
ETTh1       & $0.483 \pm 0.019$ & $\mathit{0.453}$ & $\mathit{0.416}$ & $+6.6\%$ & $+16.1\%$ \\
\textbf{ETTh2}       & $\mathbf{0.426 \pm 0.077}$ & $\mathit{0.452}$ & $\mathit{0.341}$ & $\mathbf{-5.7\%}$ & $+25.0\%$ \\
ETTm1       & $0.370 \pm 0.014$ & $\mathit{0.358}$ & $\mathit{0.322}$ & $+3.4\%$ & $+15.0\%$ \\
\textbf{ETTm2}       & $\mathbf{0.207 \pm 0.004}$ & $\mathit{0.252}$ & $\mathit{0.200}$ & $\mathbf{-17.9\%}$ & $+3.4\%$ \\
Weather     & $0.209 \pm 0.007$ & $\mathit{0.198}$ & $\mathit{0.208}$ & $+5.5\%$ & $+0.5\%$ \\
\textbf{Electricity} & $\mathbf{0.170 \pm 0.002}$ & $\mathit{0.171}$ & $\mathit{0.158}$ & $\mathbf{-0.7\%}$ & $+7.5\%$ \\
\midrule
\textbf{Mean}        & --- & --- & --- & $\mathbf{-1.5\%}$ & $\mathbf{+11.2\%}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{Honest interpretation.} Across the 6 datasets, the Raw-MLP MoE slightly outperforms Dual-Stream on average ($-1.5\%$), winning 3/6 datasets outright (ETTh2: $-5.7\%$; ETTm2: $-17.9\%$; Electricity: $-0.7\%$). It loses by $3$--$7\%$ on the three datasets with complex temporal dynamics (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather). The TSFM is therefore not adding universal value to the gap-closing result; it adds dataset-dependent, modest value where complex dynamics exist, and contributes essentially nothing on the linear-dominated datasets.

\textbf{Routing entropy diagnostics.} In all $30$ Raw-MLP MoE runs, routing entropy saturates near $\log K = 1.609$ (range $1.20$--$1.60$, mean $\approx 1.49$, i.e., $93\%$ of the uniform maximum), indicating the router degenerates to near-uniform mixing. Per-dataset means lie in $[1.31, 1.60]$: highest on Electricity ($1.60$, $99\%$ of $\log K$), lowest on ETTh2 ($1.31$, $81\%$). This means the Raw-MLP MoE is operating as a learned ensemble of $5$ size-diverse MLPs rather than as a per-sample mixture of specialists; the diversity comes from the heterogeneous expert capacities rather than from per-window routing decisions. \textbf{Contrast with main RR-MoA.} Under the same $K{=}5$, Top-$2$, raw-router protocol but \emph{with the frozen MOMENT-small backbone present}, main RR-MoA achieves mean entropy $1.34$ on the same six datasets ($83\%$ of $\log K$, $0.15$~nats lower than the backbone-free version), with per-dataset means spanning $[1.11, 1.44]$ (per-seed values span the broader $[1.0, 1.57]$ range cited in the abstract; per-run logs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/rrmoa\_entropy/}). The two most specialized routers (per-dataset mean) are on ETTh2 ($1.11$, $69\%$ of $\log K$) and ETTm2 ($1.18$, $73\%$); the least specialized on Electricity ($1.44$, $90\%$). Removing the backbone shifts entropy uniformly toward $\log K$ across all six datasets, quantifying the difference between ``MoE-as-ensemble'' (no backbone) and ``MoE-as-specialist-mixture'' (with backbone): the backbone induces enough hidden-state structure that the same Conv1d gate, on the same raw input, makes substantively non-uniform routing decisions. Figure~\ref{fig:routing_viz} corroborates this with expert-assignment scatter against amplitude/volatility quartiles.

\textbf{What this does and does not say about the paper's core claims.} This finding does \emph{not} undermine the central diagnosis of the paper: normalization-induced routing collapse on hidden-state routers (AdaMix) is a real, mechanistically-explained phenomenon, predicted by Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} for any TSFM with internal RevIN and verified empirically on MOMENT-small/large; pre-normalization routing (RR-MoA) is the causal fix at the router input. That diagnosis is independent of whether the MOMENT backbone is the most efficient architecture for forecasting on every dataset: the mechanism predicts the same failure mode for any backbone with internal instance normalization that satisfies the bound's premise. What this finding \emph{does} say is that the dual-stream gap-closing story should be reframed: rather than ``the TSFM has complementary nonlinear features that dual-stream extracts,'' the more accurate reading is ``the TSFM contributes complementary signal on datasets with nonlinear dynamics (where it wins by $1$--$6\%$), and contributes essentially nothing on datasets where DLinear is already near-optimal (where pure raw-MLP MoE matches or beats it).''

\textbf{Bottom line.} Resolving the confound stated at the start of this section: the TSFM is dead weight on ETTh2, ETTm2, and Electricity, where the backbone-free Raw-MLP MoE wins by $0.7$--$17.9\%$ over Dual-Stream, and contributes $3$--$6\%$ MSE on ETTh1, ETTm1, and Weather, where Dual-Stream retains the advantage. The dataset-conditional contribution of the TSFM is itself an empirical pattern worth flagging to the TSFM-design community: \emph{frozen TSFMs are useful where forecasting requires nonlinear temporal modeling beyond what a 5-MLP ensemble can express, and unnecessary where it does not}. The single most important consequence for our paper is that the routing-collapse diagnosis (the core scientific contribution) is robust to whether the TSFM is the best backbone: it explains the AdaMix-collapse phenomenon and predicts the cross-backbone behavior regardless.

\end{document}
```

\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\textbf{Honest interpretation.} Across the 6 datasets, the Raw-MLP MoE slightly outperforms Dual-Stream on average ($-1.5\%$), winning 3/6 datasets outright (ETTh2: $-5.7\%$; ETTm2: $-17.9\%$; Electricity: $-0.7\%$). It loses by $3$--$7\%$ on the three datasets with complex temporal dynamics (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather). The TSFM is therefore not adding universal value to the gap-closing result; it adds dataset-dependent, modest value where complex dynamics exist, and contributes essentially nothing on the linear-dominated datasets.

\textbf{Routing entropy diagnostics.} In all $30$ Raw-MLP MoE runs, routing entropy saturates near $\log K = 1.609$ (range $1.20$--$1.60$, mean $\approx 1.49$, i.e., $93\%$ of the uniform maximum), indicating the router degenerates to near-uniform mixing. Per-dataset means lie in $[1.31, 1.60]$: highest on Electricity ($1.60$, $99\%$ of $\log K$), lowest on ETTh2 ($1.31$, $81\%$). This means the Raw-MLP MoE is operating as a learned ensemble of $5$ size-diverse MLPs rather than as a per-sample mixture of specialists; the diversity comes from the heterogeneous expert capacities rather than from per-window routing decisions. \textbf{Contrast with main RR-MoA.} Under the same $K{=}5$, Top-$2$, raw-router protocol but \emph{with the frozen MOMENT-small backbone present}, main RR-MoA achieves mean entropy $1.34$ on the same six datasets ($83\%$ of $\log K$, $0.15$~nats lower than the backbone-free version), with per-dataset means spanning $[1.11, 1.44]$ (per-seed values span the broader $[1.0, 1.57]$ range cited in the abstract; per-run logs in \texttt{evidence\_vm/rrmoa\_entropy/}). The two most specialized routers (per-dataset mean) are on ETTh2 ($1.11$, $69\%$ of $\log K$) and ETTm2 ($1.18$, $73\%$); the least specialized on Electricity ($1.44$, $90\%$). Removing the backbone shifts entropy uniformly toward $\log K$ across all six datasets, quantifying the difference between ``MoE-as-ensemble'' (no backbone) and ``MoE-as-specialist-mixture'' (with backbone): the backbone induces enough hidden-state structure that the same Conv1d gate, on the same raw input, makes substantively non-uniform routing decisions. Figure~\ref{fig:routing_viz} corroborates this with expert-assignment scatter against amplitude/volatility quartiles.

\textbf{What this does and does not say about the paper's core claims.} This finding does \emph{not} undermine the central diagnosis of the paper: normalization-induced routing collapse on hidden-state routers (AdaMix) is a real, mechanistically-explained phenomenon, predicted by Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} for any TSFM with internal RevIN and verified empirically on MOMENT-small/large; pre-normalization routing (RR-MoA) is the causal fix at the router input. That diagnosis is independent of whether the MOMENT backbone is the most efficient architecture for forecasting on every dataset: the mechanism predicts the same failure mode for any backbone with internal instance normalization that satisfies the bound's premise. What this finding \emph{does} say is that the dual-stream gap-closing story should be reframed: rather than ``the TSFM has complementary nonlinear features that dual-stream extracts,'' the more accurate reading is ``the TSFM contributes complementary signal on datasets with nonlinear dynamics (where it wins by $1$--$6\%$), and contributes essentially nothing on datasets where DLinear is already near-optimal (where pure raw-MLP MoE matches or beats it).''

\textbf{Bottom line.} Resolving the confound stated at the start of this section: the TSFM is dead weight on ETTh2, ETTm2, and Electricity, where the backbone-free Raw-MLP MoE wins by $0.7$--$17.9\%$ over Dual-Stream, and contributes $3$--$6\%$ MSE on ETTh1, ETTm1, and Weather, where Dual-Stream retains the advantage. The dataset-conditional contribution of the TSFM is itself an empirical pattern worth flagging to the TSFM-design community: \emph{frozen TSFMs are useful where forecasting requires nonlinear temporal modeling beyond what a 5-MLP ensemble can express, and unnecessary where it does not}. The single most important consequence for our paper is that the routing-collapse diagnosis (the core scientific contribution) is robust to whether the TSFM is the best backbone: it explains the AdaMix-collapse phenomenon and predicts the cross-backbone behavior regardless.

\end{document}

 \textbf{Bottom line.} Resolving the confound stated at the start of this section: the TSFM is dead weight on ETTh2, ETTm2, and Electricity, where the backbone-free Raw-MLP MoE wins by $0.7$--$17.9\%$ over Dual-Stream, and contributes $3$--$6\%$ MSE on ETTh1, ETTm1, and Weather, where Dual-Stream retains the advantage. The dataset-conditional contribution of the TSFM is itself an empirical pattern worth flagging to the TSFM-design community: \emph{frozen TSFMs are useful where forecasting requires nonlinear temporal modeling beyond what a 5-MLP ensemble can express, and unnecessary where it does not}. The single most important consequence for our paper is that the routing-collapse diagnosis (the core scientific contribution) is robust to whether the TSFM is the best backbone: it explains the AdaMix-collapse phenomenon and predicts the cross-backbone behavior regardless.
